CISA KEV: CVE-2025-59689 — Libraesva Email Security Gateway Command Injection Vulnerability

CISA KEV
2025-09-29 1 use case 0 techniques 3 kill-chain phases detected

Libraesva Email Security Gateway (ESG) contains a command injection vulnerability which allows command injection via a compressed e-mail attachment. Vendor: Libraesva, Product: Email Security Gateway. Federal patch due: 2025-10-20.

Actions on Objectives
—
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code.
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s) Reconnaissance High
Match vulnerability scanner output to the CVE(s) named in the article.
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-59689")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-59689")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

Critical

CISA KEV: CVE-2025-10035 — Fortra GoAnywhere MFT Deserialization of Untrusted Data Vulnerability

CISA KEV
2025-09-29 6 use cases 1 technique 5 kill-chain phases detected

Fortra GoAnywhere MFT contains a deserialization of untrusted data vulnerability allows an actor with a validly forged license response signature to deserialize an arbitrary actor-controlled object, possibly leading to command injection. Vendor: Fortra, Product: GoAnywhere MFT. Known ransomware use: Known. Federal patch due: 2025-10-20.

Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change Actions on Objectives Medium
Threshold detection: many files renamed in short window, often with new extension.
ATT&CKT1486
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem DeviceFileEvents
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(FileName) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, bin(Timestamp, 1m)
| where files > 200    // empirical: > 200 unique-file renames in 1m by one account on one host
                       //            is well above the P99 of legitimate bulk-tooling
| order by files desc

ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where EventType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(TargetFileName)
            by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, bin(TimeGenerated, 1m)
| where files > 200    // P99 of legit bulk-tooling per-minute rename volume
| order by files desc
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
| tstats summariesonly=false count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) Actions on Objectives High
Mimikatz, comsvcs.dll MiniDump, or any non-Windows process opening LSASS.
ATT&CKT1003.001 T1003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceEvents
DeviceEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType == "OpenProcessApiCall"
| where FileName =~ "lsass.exe"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsSense.exe","MsMpEng.exe","csrss.exe",
                                          "svchost.exe","wininit.exe","services.exe",
                                          "lsm.exe","SearchProtocolHost.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFolderPath, AccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
// SecurityEvent EventID 4663 = object access. AccessMask 0x10 / 0x40 / 0x1010
// indicate read access to lsass.exe — credential-dump signature. Audit
// Object Access > Audit File System policy required.
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4663
| where ObjectName endswith "\lsass.exe"
| where Account !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ObjectName, ProcessName,
          AccessMask, AccessReason
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: LSASS Process Access Outside Microsoft-Signed Tooling
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d04
status: experimental
description: |
    Anything opening lsass.exe with read-memory access that isn't a
    Microsoft signed component (Defender, csrss, etc.) is suspect.
    Mimikatz / Pypykatz / nanodump signature.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1003/001/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1003.001
    - attack.credential-access
logsource:
    category: process_access
    product: windows
detection:
    selection:
        TargetImage|endswith: '\lsass.exe'
    filter_known_good:
        SourceImage|endswith:
            - '\MsSense.exe'
            - '\MsMpEng.exe'
            - '\csrss.exe'
            - '\svchost.exe'
            - '\wininit.exe'
            - '\services.exe'
            - '\lsm.exe'
            - '\SearchProtocolHost.exe'
    condition: selection and not filter_known_good
falsepositives:
    - Third-party EDR / forensic tools — extend the filter list
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where (Processes.process="*lsass*" OR Processes.process="*sekurlsa*"
        OR Processes.process="*MiniDump*" OR Processes.process="*comsvcs.dll*MiniDump*"
        OR Processes.process="*procdump*lsass*")
       OR (Processes.process_name="rundll32.exe" AND Processes.process="*comsvcs*MiniDump*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where (Processes.process="*lsass*" OR Processes.process="*sekurlsa*"
        OR Processes.process="*MiniDump*" OR Processes.process="*comsvcs.dll*MiniDump*"
        OR Processes.process="*procdump*lsass*")
       OR (Processes.process_name="rundll32.exe" AND Processes.process="*comsvcs*MiniDump*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement Actions on Objectives High
psexec / paexec / smbexec / wmic /node — service install over SMB from remote host.
ATT&CKT1021.002 T1569.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where FileName in~ ("psexec.exe","psexesvc.exe","paexec.exe","smbexec.py")
   or (FileName =~ "wmic.exe" and ProcessCommandLine has "/node:")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by Timestamp desc

SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688            // process-create (audit-policy required)
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\psexec.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\psexesvc.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\paexec.exe"
   or (NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe" and CommandLine has "/node:")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: PsExec / SMB Lateral Movement Tooling Execution
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d03
status: experimental
description: |
    PsExec, paexec, smbexec, csexec, remcom — admin-grade tooling that
    pivots over SMB. Pair with `wmic /node:` for the full cluster.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1021/002/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1569/002/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1021.002
    - attack.t1569.002
    - attack.lateral-movement
    - attack.execution
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_psexec:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\psexec.exe'
            - '\psexec64.exe'
            - '\psexesvc.exe'
            - '\paexec.exe'
            - '\csexec.exe'
            - '\remcom.exe'
            - '\smbexec.py'
    selection_wmic_remote:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\wmic.exe'
        CommandLine|contains:
            - '/node:'
    condition: selection_psexec or selection_wmic_remote
falsepositives:
    - Sysadmins running approved remote-management tooling — wrap with
      a known-admin-host watchlist
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.process_name IN ("psexec.exe","psexesvc.exe","paexec.exe","smbexec.py")
       OR (Processes.process_name="wmic.exe" AND Processes.process="*/node:*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.process_name IN ("psexec.exe","psexesvc.exe","paexec.exe","smbexec.py")
       OR (Processes.process_name="wmic.exe" AND Processes.process="*/node:*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
Network connections to article IPs / domains Command & Control High
Outbound traffic to attacker infrastructure named in the article.
ATT&CKT1071
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic Web Network_Resolution.DNS DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where RemoteIP in ("31.220.45.120", "45.11.183.123", "213.183.63.41") or RemoteUrl has_any ("")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, RemoteIP, RemotePort, RemoteUrl,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
let BadIPs     = dynamic(["31.220.45.120", "45.11.183.123", "213.183.63.41"]);
let BadDomains = dynamic([""]);
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where DstIpAddr in (BadIPs)
   or Url has_any (BadDomains)
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber, Url, NetworkProtocol
| order by TimeGenerated desc

File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match Installation High
Match SHA256/SHA1/MD5 named in the article against EDR file/process telemetry.
ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("4106c35ff46bb6f2f4a42d63a2b8a619f1e1df72414122ddf6fd1b1a644b3220", "c7e2632702d0e22598b90ea226d3cde4830455d9232bd8b33ebcb13827e99bc3", "cd5aa589873d777c6e919c4438afe8bceccad6bbe57739e2ccb70b39aee1e8b3", "5ba7de7d5115789b952d9b1c6cff440c9128f438de933ff9044a68fff8496d19") or SHA1 in~ ("4106c35ff46bb6f2f4a42d63a2b8a619f1e1df72414122ddf6fd1b1a644b3220", "c7e2632702d0e22598b90ea226d3cde4830455d9232bd8b33ebcb13827e99bc3", "cd5aa589873d777c6e919c4438afe8bceccad6bbe57739e2ccb70b39aee1e8b3", "5ba7de7d5115789b952d9b1c6cff440c9128f438de933ff9044a68fff8496d19") or MD5 in~ ("4106c35ff46bb6f2f4a42d63a2b8a619f1e1df72414122ddf6fd1b1a644b3220", "c7e2632702d0e22598b90ea226d3cde4830455d9232bd8b33ebcb13827e99bc3", "cd5aa589873d777c6e919c4438afe8bceccad6bbe57739e2ccb70b39aee1e8b3", "5ba7de7d5115789b952d9b1c6cff440c9128f438de933ff9044a68fff8496d19")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("4106c35ff46bb6f2f4a42d63a2b8a619f1e1df72414122ddf6fd1b1a644b3220", "c7e2632702d0e22598b90ea226d3cde4830455d9232bd8b33ebcb13827e99bc3", "cd5aa589873d777c6e919c4438afe8bceccad6bbe57739e2ccb70b39aee1e8b3", "5ba7de7d5115789b952d9b1c6cff440c9128f438de933ff9044a68fff8496d19") or SHA1 in~ ("4106c35ff46bb6f2f4a42d63a2b8a619f1e1df72414122ddf6fd1b1a644b3220", "c7e2632702d0e22598b90ea226d3cde4830455d9232bd8b33ebcb13827e99bc3", "cd5aa589873d777c6e919c4438afe8bceccad6bbe57739e2ccb70b39aee1e8b3", "5ba7de7d5115789b952d9b1c6cff440c9128f438de933ff9044a68fff8496d19") or MD5 in~ ("4106c35ff46bb6f2f4a42d63a2b8a619f1e1df72414122ddf6fd1b1a644b3220", "c7e2632702d0e22598b90ea226d3cde4830455d9232bd8b33ebcb13827e99bc3", "cd5aa589873d777c6e919c4438afe8bceccad6bbe57739e2ccb70b39aee1e8b3", "5ba7de7d5115789b952d9b1c6cff440c9128f438de933ff9044a68fff8496d19")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = ProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceProcessEvents" )
| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["4106c35ff46bb6f2f4a42d63a2b8a619f1e1df72414122ddf6fd1b1a644b3220", "c7e2632702d0e22598b90ea226d3cde4830455d9232bd8b33ebcb13827e99bc3", "cd5aa589873d777c6e919c4438afe8bceccad6bbe57739e2ccb70b39aee1e8b3", "5ba7de7d5115789b952d9b1c6cff440c9128f438de933ff9044a68fff8496d19"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: File or Process Matching Article Hash IOCs
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d0b
status: experimental
description: |
    Replace `__HASH_LIST__` placeholder with article-specific SHA256
    values. Use a watchlist or external indicator file for management
    at scale.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1027/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1027
    - attack.defense-evasion
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection:
        Hashes|contains:
            - 'SHA256=__HASH_PLACEHOLDER_REPLACE_ME__'

CISA KEV: CVE-2025-20352 — Cisco IOS and IOS XE Software SNMP Denial of Service and Remote Code Execution Vulnerability

CISA KEV
2025-09-29 2 use cases 1 technique 3 kill-chain phases detected

Cisco IOS and IOS XE contains a stack-based buffer overflow vulnerability in the Simple Network Management Protocol (SNMP) subsystem that could allow for denial of service or remote code execution. A successful exploit could allow a low-privileged attacker to cause the affected system to reload, resulting in a DoS condition, or allow a high-privileged attacker to execute arbitrary code as the root user and obtain full control of the affected system. Vendor: Cisco, Product: IOS and IOS XE. Federal patch due: 2025-10-20.

// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-20352", "CVE-2017-3881")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-20352", "CVE-2017-3881")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,

| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the

let BadHashes = dynamic(["2abc874435c16aa5cfd431b0d9c26095ef4b9429bd82306f054c367e96df49b2", "69d761bdde73ea8e33384cf986d7e9c2d9011f7aad8933e8af64e60a77091e11", "b08877f6f1c6c097240a6a8aa4a23243e3b14a1432170bc3fa5fa9886a2b19b4", "9b8a896aa2057f46e17b18bbe091d85fb816b1d3232a3178d6aba94df3a92f6a", "81b35152768f28a479ba9f7e27d66042b0d7edcd79355481aa401f3f47a7733b", "3a524bc40ca7c11b68283504f0119caeefd7589edea621d43d5d0cd973354675", "e303d0c6c59b4dc55edc0212a9319702e9db7fa03185ae9177777b874c02d4c1", "7cc7aed51adb426e55d82fd74c55b78f6ecbb895a315be721ef149a17f4b3a9b", "235dc2d8c92661e5e2797a03bccd2653272ca1ac93401d194d7784930ca17a5a"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2021-21311")

| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

High

Malicious MCP Server on npm postmark-mcp Harvests Emails

Snyk
2025-09-25 6 use cases 2 techniques 6 kill-chain phases detected

Urgent security alert: On September 25, 2025, the npm package 'postmark-mcp' was compromised, secretly exfiltrating email contents. Learn about the incident timeline, impact, and immediate mitigation steps, including uninstalling, rotating credentials, and scanning with Snyk's MCP-Scan.

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children · Article-specific behavioural hunt — Malicious MCP Server on npm postmark-mcp Harvests Emails
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Installation or presence of malicious postmark-mcp npm package (v1.0.16+)
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] DNS or HTTP egress to giftshop.club exfil domain · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] Outbound email BCC'd to giftshop.club exfil domain (postmark-mcp backdoor)
Outbound email BCC'd to giftshop.club exfil domain (postmark-mcp backdoor) AIActions on Objectives High
Detects any outbound message that contains a BCC, CC, or recipient at giftshop.club — the hard-coded exfil sink injected into postmark-mcp v1.0.16-1.0.18. Every email sent through the compromised MCP server silently appends phan@giftshop.club as BCC. Rationale: BCC to phan@giftshop.club is the literal exfil mechanism named in the Snyk advisory and the only code diff between v1.0.15 (benign) and v1.0.18 (malicious). Any tenant email landing at this domain is the exfil event itself, not a precursor — extremely high fidelity. Cross-checked: Snyk advisory, npm registry removal of postmark-mcp, ActiveCampaign/postmark-mcp upstream repo. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/malicious-mcp-server-on-npm-postmark-mcp-harvests-emails/ • https://github.com/ActiveCampaign/postmark-mcp • https://www.npmjs.com/~phanpak
ATT&CKT1567 T1114.003
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate business correspondence to a real recipient whose mail server happens to be hosted under giftshop.club (none known at time of writing — domain is currently associated only with the exfil sink)
  • Phishing-simulation tooling pre-seeded with the IOC domain for training
  • Threat-intel platforms that auto-deliver IOC reports to abuse@giftshop.club
  • Security researcher mailbox correspondence about the incident itself
  • Sandbox / EDR detonation of the malicious package within the corp environment
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: Pull all matching messages, identify sending mailbox(es), confirm whether the agent/automation tied to that mailbox has postmark-mcp installed (`npm ls postmark-mcp` on each host, search package-lock.json across repos). Inspect Subject + InternetMessageId to scope which downstream recipients also received the legitimate copy. Step 2 — Contain: Block giftshop.club at the mail gateway and egress proxy, revoke the Postmark API token used by the affected agent, uninstall postmark-mcp on every host (`npm uninstall -g postmark-mcp` and remove from project deps). Step 3 — Hunt: Pull 30-day message-trace for any giftshop.club recipient, identify all credentials/links/PII in the exfiltrated body content (password resets, API tokens, invoices), rotate every credential that appeared in those messages, and check npm audit for other phanpak-authored packages in the environment.

   or AdditionalFields has "giftshop.club"
| project Timestamp, NetworkMessageId, InternetMessageId, SenderFromAddress, SenderMailFromAddress, RecipientEmailAddress, Subject, EmailDirection, DeliveryAction, DeliveryLocation, SenderIPv4
| order by Timestamp desc

| where OfficeWorkload =~ "Exchange"
| where Recipients has "giftshop.club" or P2RecipientList has "giftshop.club" or Sender has "giftshop.club"
| project TimeGenerated, Operation, UserId, ClientIP, Sender, P2Sender, Recipients, P2RecipientList, Subject, MessageId, InternetMessageId
| order by TimeGenerated desc

DNS or HTTP egress to giftshop.club exfil domain AICommand & Control High
Network-layer detection for any host reaching giftshop.club — covers the Postmark API call path that secretly attaches the BCC. Catches hosts where postmark-mcp is installed but no email has actually shipped yet, or where mail telemetry isn't centralised. Rationale: giftshop.club is the only network IOC named in the Snyk advisory and is the C2/exfil endpoint receiving the BCC mail. Per-table column corrections: DnsEvents uses Name+Computer+ClientIP, ImDnsActivity uses DnsQuery+DvcHostname+SrcIpAddr, CommonSecurityLog uses DestinationHostName+SourceHostName+RequestURL — none of these tables expose IPAddresses or DeviceName. Cross-checked: Snyk advisory and npm registry takedown. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/malicious-mcp-server-on-npm-postmark-mcp-harvests-emails/ • https://www.npmjs.com/~phanpak
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1567
Data sourcesNetwork_Resolution.DNS Web.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Threat-intel feed retrieval that includes giftshop.club as a sample IOC and the test scanner resolves it
  • Security researcher hosts deliberately resolving the domain to study sinkhole state
  • Sandbox detonation lab traffic
  • Endpoint browsing of the Snyk advisory page that embeds the bracketed defanged domain (most browsers won't resolve giftshop[.]club, but some link-preview agents do)
  • DNS resolver cache-warming behaviour on shared infra
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("snyk.exe","mcp-scan.exe") and InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "sandbox"
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: Identify the initiating process on the resolving host (Defender InitiatingProcessFileName / Falcon ContextImageFileName); if it's node.exe or an MCP runtime, confirm postmark-mcp is in that process's working directory. Step 2 — Contain: Sinkhole giftshop.club at the resolver and block at egress, isolate the offending host if EDR confirms postmark-mcp is loaded, and revoke any Postmark API tokens scoped to that host. Step 3 — Hunt: Pivot on the parent process tree to find which agentic workflow invoked the MCP server, enumerate every node_modules folder org-wide for postmark-mcp@>=1.0.16, and review 30-day mail logs for outbound BCCs to giftshop.club to scope exfiltration.

Installation or presence of malicious postmark-mcp npm package (v1.0.16+) AIInstallation High
File-system detection for postmark-mcp arriving on disk via npm install, yarn add, or repo clone — catches the package before its first email exfil. Targets the package's index.js / package.json under any node_modules path. Rationale: postmark-mcp is the literal compromised npm package name from the Snyk advisory; v1.0.16+ are the malicious releases. Any install/exec is a high-confidence indicator that the host is shipping email-harvesting code, even before the first BCC fires. Cross-checked against the npm registry takedown and the Snyk advisory. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/malicious-mcp-server-on-npm-postmark-mcp-harvests-emails/ • https://www.npmjs.com/~phanpak • https://github.com/invariantlabs-ai/mcp-scan
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1059.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security researcher / blue-team host deliberately installing postmark-mcp to study the backdoor
  • Snyk MCP-Scan or similar tooling string-matching the package name in scan output
  • Air-gapped CI test environments validating supply-chain detections
  • Forensic triage workstation cloning the upstream ActiveCampaign/postmark-mcp repo (note: upstream is unrelated to the malicious npm package — same name, different supply chain)
  • Developer running `npm view postmark-mcp` for incident-response triage
Suppression filter
| where ProcessCommandLine !has_any ("snyk test","mcp-scan","npm audit","npm view","npm info")
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: Inspect the package version actually installed (`npm ls postmark-mcp --all` on host, or check package-lock.json) — confirm it's >=1.0.16 (1.0.16-1.0.18 are the known-malicious range). If <1.0.16 or absent from npm, downgrade priority. Step 2 — Contain: Uninstall the package from every host and CI runner, purge node_modules and the npm cache (`npm cache clean --force`), rotate the Postmark server token and any SMTP credentials the agent used, and block the package name in the internal npm registry / repo policy. Step 3 — Hunt: Inventory other npm packages published by user phanpak across the org and treat them as untrusted until reviewed; check 30-day mail logs for BCC to giftshop.club; pivot to the UC #2 network detection to confirm whether exfil has actually occurred.

  | project Timestamp, DeviceName, DeviceId, ActionType, FolderPath, FileName, SHA256, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName
),

  | where Timestamp > ago(30d)

     or FileName in~ ("npm.exe","npm.cmd","yarn.cmd","pnpm.exe","npx.cmd","npx.exe")

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access

    category: process_creation
    product: windows

    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium

Shai-Hulud bundle.js postinstall payload by known SHA256 hash AIInstallation Medium
Detects file write or process load of the malicious npm postinstall payload bundle.js with the SHA256 hash published by Snyk/Unit42/Wiz for the Shai-Hulud worm. This is the most authoritative IOC for any host that installed a compromised ngx-bootstrap / ng2-file-upload / @ctrl/tinycolor / @ctrl/* / @nativescript-community/* / @crowdstrike/* package. Rationale: Hash 46faab8ab153fae6e80e7cca38eab363075bb524edd79e42269217a083628f09 was published by Snyk for the bundle.js payload and corroborated by Unit42 and Wiz as the canonical Shai-Hulud postinstall blob. Hash-based detection is the cleanest possible TP signal — any hit is the actual worm payload landing on disk or being loaded. Cross-checked against: • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/npm-supply-chain-attack/ • https://www.wiz.io/blog/shai-hulud-npm-supply-chain-attack • https://www.sysdig.com/blog/shai-hulud-the-novel-self-replicating-worm-infecting-hundreds-of-npm-packages
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1059.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security researchers intentionally downloading the IOC sample into a sandbox for analysis
  • SOC analysts writing the hash into threat-intel notes / IOC docs that get indexed by file scanners
  • Backup or DR replication re-introducing the file from a snapshot taken pre-eradication
  • Vulnerability-scanner / SBOM tool fetching the unpublished tarball for analysis
Suppression filter
| where not(FolderPath has_any ("\\malware-analysis\\", "\\sandbox\\", "\\IOC-samples\\")) and not(InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("defender.exe", "MsMpEng.exe", "trufflehog.exe"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the host installed an affected package version (check package-lock.json, pnpm-lock.yaml, yarn.lock for ngx-bootstrap@18.1.4/19.0.3/20.0.3-20.0.6, ng2-file-upload, @ctrl/tinycolor, @ctrl/*, @nativescript-community/*, @crowdstrike/* installed on/after 2025-09-15). Pull DeviceProcessEvents around the file event for parent npm/node context. 2) Contain: isolate host; revoke all npm, GitHub PAT/SSH, and cloud (AWS/GCP/Azure) credentials touched on the host within the last 30 days from a CLEAN workstation per GitHub Advisory GHSA-6m4g-vm7c-f8w6. 3) Hunt: pivot on aid/DeviceId for outbound webhook.site traffic (UC2), TruffleHog execution (UC3), and shai-hulud-workflow.yml drops (UC4); audit user's GitHub org for new public repos named 'Shai-Hulud' or workflow files named shai-hulud-workflow.yml.
let ShaiHuludBundleHash = "46faab8ab153fae6e80e7cca38eab363075bb524edd79e42269217a083628f09";

  (DeviceFileEvents | where Timestamp > ago(30d) | where SHA256 == ShaiHuludBundleHash | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName),
  (DeviceImageLoadEvents | where Timestamp > ago(30d) | where SHA256 == ShaiHuludBundleHash | project Timestamp, DeviceName, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName=tostring(InitiatingProcessAccountName))
| order by Timestamp desc

Shai-Hulud worm C2 exfiltration to webhook.site UUID bb8ca5f6 AICommand & Control High
Detects outbound HTTP/DNS to the specific webhook.site endpoint hardcoded in the Shai-Hulud worm (UUID bb8ca5f6-4175-45d2-b042-fc9ebb8170b7) used to exfiltrate stolen npm/GitHub/cloud credentials. The UUID path is the unique discriminator — webhook.site itself has legitimate dev use, but this UUID is exclusive to the attacker's drop point. Rationale: The UUID path bb8ca5f6-4175-45d2-b042-fc9ebb8170b7 is hardcoded in the bundle.js payload (confirmed by Checkmarx, Unit42, Sysdig) and is the worm's sole exfil endpoint. Matching on the UUID rather than the bare hostname keeps FP rate near zero since webhook.site is a popular dev tool but no legitimate user shares that specific UUID. Cross-checked against: • https://checkmarx.com/zero-post/npm-hit-by-shai-hulud-the-self-replicating-supply-chain-attack/ • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/npm-supply-chain-attack/ • https://www.sysdig.com/blog/shai-hulud-the-novel-self-replicating-worm-infecting-hundreds-of-npm-packages
ATT&CKT1567 T1071.001
Data sourcesWeb Network_Traffic.All_Traffic Network_Resolution.DNS
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Threat-intel analyst manually fetching the IOC URL for analysis from an internal sandbox
  • DFIR responders intentionally curling the endpoint to verify it's still live
  • SOC playbook documentation indexed by web crawlers / proxies that triggers on the URL string in a doc
  • Honey-token / canary tool that intentionally pings known-bad endpoints
Suppression filter
| where not(InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe", "msedge.exe", "firefox.exe")) or InitiatingProcessParentFileName has_any ("node.exe", "npm.exe", "pnpm.exe", "yarn.exe", "trufflehog.exe")
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm source host installed an affected npm package version (lockfile review) and pull DeviceProcessEvents in the 5-minute window around the egress for parent node/npm/CI-runner context. 2) Contain: block webhook.site at proxy/firewall organization-wide (low-risk — most enterprises do not require legitimate webhook.site access from production); isolate calling host; immediately revoke all GitHub PATs, npm tokens, AWS/GCP/Azure keys, and SSH keys owned by the calling user identity. 3) Hunt: enumerate the user's GitHub org for unexpected public repos (especially named 'Shai-Hulud') and new GitHub Actions workflow files in any repo they have push access to; check npm registry account for unauthorized package publishes in the last 7 days.

  (CommonSecurityLog
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
    | where (DestinationHostName has "webhook.site" and RequestURL has ShaiHuludWebhookPath) or RequestURL has ShaiHuludWebhookPath
    | project TimeGenerated, SourceIP, SourceUserName, DestinationHostName, RequestURL, DeviceVendor, DeviceProduct, RequestClientApplication),
  (ImWebSession
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
    | where Url has ShaiHuludWebhookPath
    | project TimeGenerated, SrcHostname=DvcHostname, SrcUsername, DstHostname=HttpHostName, Url, HttpMethod, EventVendor, EventProduct),
  (ImDnsActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
    | where DnsQuery =~ "webhook.site" or DnsQuery endswith ".webhook.site"
    | project TimeGenerated, SrcHostname, SrcUsername, DnsQuery, DnsResponseName, EventVendor, EventProduct)
| order by TimeGenerated desc

TruffleHog secret-scanner executed by node/npm postinstall context AIActions on Objectives High
Detects the TruffleHog binary being executed as a child of node/npm/pnpm/yarn — the Shai-Hulud worm uses TruffleHog at install time to harvest secrets from the dev/CI filesystem before exfiltrating them. Standalone TruffleHog runs by developers are usually invoked from a terminal/shell, not from node within an install context. Rationale: Multiple vendor write-ups (Wiz, Sysdig, ReversingLabs, Zscaler) confirm Shai-Hulud invokes TruffleHog from its bundle.js payload to enumerate AWS/GCP/Azure/npm/GitHub secrets. The parent-process predicate (node/npm) is the critical discriminator: legitimate TruffleHog usage is from CI YAML/shell, not from inside a node install context. Cross-checked against: • https://www.wiz.io/blog/shai-hulud-npm-supply-chain-attack • https://www.zscaler.com/blogs/security-research/mitigating-risks-shai-hulud-npm-worm • https://www.sysdig.com/blog/shai-hulud-the-novel-self-replicating-worm-infecting-hundreds-of-npm-packages
ATT&CKT1552.001 T1083 T1059.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Self-hosted CI runner where a Node-based pipeline orchestrator (e.g. an npm-wrapped CI script) deliberately invokes TruffleHog
  • Developer authoring a custom secret-scanning npm package who tests it locally
  • Security-team rollout of an internal npm-distributed pre-commit hook that wraps TruffleHog
  • Renovate/Dependabot bot using a node-based image that bundles trufflehog for advisory scanning
Suppression filter
| where not(InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("truffleSecurity/trufflehog-action", "actions/trufflehog", "semgrep") and InitiatingProcessFolderPath has_any ("\\actions-runner\\", "/_work/", "/runner/"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the parent npm command line — confirm whether the install corresponds to one of the compromised package versions (ngx-bootstrap@18.1.4/19.0.3/20.0.3-20.0.6, ng2-file-upload, @ctrl/tinycolor, @ctrl/*, @nativescript-community/*, @crowdstrike/*). Check the trufflehog binary's parent folder — Shai-Hulud drops it into a transient temp path, not /usr/local/bin. 2) Contain: kill the trufflehog and parent node process; isolate the host; revoke ALL secrets present in the user's home and project dirs from a CLEAN workstation (npm, GitHub, AWS/GCP/Azure, Docker registry, SSH). 3) Hunt: correlate to webhook.site/bb8ca5f6 egress (UC2) and shai-hulud-workflow.yml creation (UC4) on the same host within ±30 min; query the user's GitHub org for new public repos named 'Shai-Hulud'.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName =~ "trufflehog" or FileName =~ "trufflehog.exe" or ProcessCommandLine has "trufflehog"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe", "node", "npm.exe", "npm-cli.js", "pnpm.exe", "yarn.exe", "npx.exe", "bash", "sh", "cmd.exe", "powershell.exe")
   and (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("node_modules", "postinstall", "bundle.js", "npm install", "npm ci", "pnpm install", "yarn install")
        or InitiatingProcessParentFileName in~ ("node.exe", "npm.exe", "pnpm.exe", "yarn.exe"))
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessParentFileName, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
union isfuzzy=true
  (ImProcessCreate
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
    | where ActingProcessName has "trufflehog" or TargetProcessName has "trufflehog" or TargetProcessCommandLine has "trufflehog"
    | where ParentProcessName has_any ("node", "npm", "pnpm", "yarn", "npx")
        or ParentProcessCommandLine has_any ("node_modules", "postinstall", "bundle.js")
    | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, TargetProcessName, TargetProcessCommandLine, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine, EventVendor),

    | where ParentProcessName has_any ("\\node.exe", "\\npm.exe", "\\pnpm.exe", "\\yarn.exe", "\\npx.exe")
    | project TimeGenerated, Computer, SubjectUserName, NewProcessName, CommandLine, ParentProcessName, ProcessId)
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Shai-Hulud persistence artifact: shai-hulud-workflow.yml file dropped on disk AIInstallation High
Detects creation of a GitHub Actions workflow file named shai-hulud-workflow.yml — the persistence artifact the worm writes into compromised repository working trees so subsequent CI runs re-execute the exfiltration payload. Filename is unique to this campaign across all vendor reports. Rationale: The workflow filename shai-hulud-workflow.yml is the named persistence IOC published by Snyk and corroborated by Checkmarx, Unit42, and Sysdig. The combination of the literal filename plus the .github/workflows/ path is uniquely diagnostic — no legitimate project uses this name. Cross-checked against: • https://checkmarx.com/zero-post/npm-hit-by-shai-hulud-the-self-replicating-supply-chain-attack/ • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/npm-supply-chain-attack/ • https://snyk.io/blog/embedded-malicious-code-in-tinycolor-and-ngx-bootstrap-releases-on-npm/
ATT&CKT1546 T1195.002 T1053.005
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security researcher cloning a worm-infected repo into a sandbox VM to analyse the workflow file
  • SOC analyst writing the IOC filename into local notes that get indexed
  • Defender / EDR test-suite recreating the file to validate this very detection
  • Internal red-team exercise simulating the worm
Suppression filter
| where not(FolderPath has_any ("\\malware-research\\", "\\sandbox\\", "\\detection-tests\\")) and not(InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("7z.exe", "WinRAR.exe") and InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("IOC", "sample"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the user account writing the file and the parent process (was it node/npm during install, or git checkout pulling an already-poisoned repo?). Read the file contents — Shai-Hulud workflow runs on push/PR and exfiltrates secrets via webhook.site. 2) Contain: delete the workflow file; isolate host; force-push remove the file from any pushed branches; revoke the GITHUB_TOKEN, repo-scoped PATs, and any GitHub Actions secrets referenced in the workflow (likely already harvested). 3) Hunt: query GitHub org audit log (workflow_run events) for any execution of the file; enumerate the user's repos for additional shai-hulud-workflow.yml drops; look for newly-created public repos named 'Shai-Hulud' in the org or under personal accounts of affected developers (these contain the exfiltrated data.json blob).
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated", "FileRenamed", "FileModified")
| where FileName =~ "shai-hulud-workflow.yml"
   or (FolderPath has @"\.github\workflows\" and FileName startswith "shai-hulud")
   or (FolderPath contains "/.github/workflows/" and FileName startswith "shai-hulud")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessParentFileName, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventType in ("FileCreated", "FileModified", "FileRenamed") or isempty(EventType)
| where TargetFileName =~ "shai-hulud-workflow.yml"
   or (TargetFilePath has ".github/workflows/" and TargetFileName startswith "shai-hulud")
   or (TargetFilePath has @"\.github\workflows\" and TargetFileName startswith "shai-hulud")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, TargetFilePath, TargetFileName, TargetFileSHA256, EventVendor, EventProduct
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Postinstall node child enumerating multiple developer credential stores AIActions on Objectives Medium
Detects a node process (child of npm/pnpm/yarn install context) reading from three or more distinct on-disk credential locations (.npmrc, .aws/credentials, .docker/config.json, .ssh/, .config/gh, .gcloud/, .kube/config) within a short window — the secrets-collection behaviour of the Shai-Hulud bundle.js before TruffleHog/webhook exfiltration. This catches variants that drop the SHA256/webhook IOCs but reuse the same enumeration pattern. Rationale: Snyk, Wiz, and Zscaler describe bundle.js enumerating npm/GitHub/cloud credential stores before exfil. Detecting the multi-path enumeration pattern (3+ distinct credential stores opened by a node child of npm install within 5 min) catches future variants that rotate the SHA256/webhook IOCs but preserve the harvesting logic — purely behavioural and IOC-agnostic. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/embedded-malicious-code-in-tinycolor-and-ngx-bootstrap-releases-on-npm/ • https://www.zscaler.com/blogs/security-research/mitigating-risks-shai-hulud-npm-worm • https://www.wiz.io/blog/shai-hulud-npm-supply-chain-attack
ATT&CKT1552.001 T1552.004 T1083
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate developer-tool npm package (e.g. AWS CDK postinstall, Docker Desktop helper) that reads multiple cloud config files at install time
  • CI pipeline that runs aws-sdk + docker-cli + gcloud-cli probe scripts in a single node-orchestrated job
  • First-time developer setup script that an organisation distributes via npm and which probes for existing credentials to seed config
  • Internal IDE plugin packaged as npm that scans for existing credentials to populate dev UI
Suppression filter
| where not(InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("aws-cdk", "@aws-amplify/cli", "firebase-tools", "@google-cloud", "gcloud-node", "docker-credential-helper")) and not(InitiatingProcessParentFileName in~ ("code.exe", "code-insiders.exe", "jetbrains-toolbox.exe"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the parent npm command and the package being installed — confirm whether it's on the Shai-Hulud affected-package list or an unknown package. Read the node child's command-line for bundle.js / postinstall references. 2) Contain: kill the node process tree; isolate host; rotate every credential type the process touched, from a clean workstation (npm token, GitHub PAT/SSH, AWS keys, GCP service-account JSON, Docker registry creds, kubeconfig). 3) Hunt: pivot on the host for webhook.site/bb8ca5f6 egress (UC2), TruffleHog spawn (UC3), shai-hulud-workflow.yml drop (UC4), or bundle.js SHA256 hit (UC1) within ±60 min — any one of those confirms Shai-Hulud over a benign installer.
let CredPaths = dynamic([".npmrc", "\\.aws\\credentials", "/.aws/credentials", "\\.docker\\config.json", "/.docker/config.json", "\\.ssh\\", "/.ssh/", "\\.config\\gh\\", "/.config/gh/", "\\.gcloud\\", "/.gcloud/", "\\.kube\\config", "/.kube/config", "\\AppData\\Roaming\\npm\\", "\\AppData\\Local\\GitHub CLI\\"]);
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated", "FileOpened", "FileModified", "FileAccessed")
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe", "node")
| where InitiatingProcessParentFileName in~ ("npm.exe", "pnpm.exe", "yarn.exe", "npx.exe", "node.exe")
   or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("node_modules", "postinstall", "bundle.js", "install")
| extend MatchedPath = tostring(parse_json(tostring(CredPaths)))
| where FolderPath has_any (CredPaths) or FileName in~ (".npmrc", "credentials", "config.json", "config")
| summarize DistinctSecrets = dcount(strcat(FolderPath, FileName)),
            SecretPaths = make_set(strcat(FolderPath, FileName), 20),
            ParentCmd = any(InitiatingProcessCommandLine),
            ParentParent = any(InitiatingProcessParentFileName),
            FirstSeen = min(Timestamp), LastSeen = max(Timestamp)
  by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessId, bin(Timestamp, 5m)
| where DistinctSecrets >= 3
| order by LastSeen desc
let CredFragments = dynamic([".npmrc", ".aws/credentials", ".aws\\credentials", ".docker/config.json", ".docker\\config.json", ".ssh/", ".ssh\\", ".config/gh/", ".config\\gh\\", ".gcloud/", ".gcloud\\", ".kube/config", ".kube\\config"]);
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ActingProcessName has_any ("node", "node.exe")
| where ActingProcessCommandLine has_any ("node_modules", "postinstall", "bundle.js", "install")
| where TargetFilePath has_any (CredFragments)
| summarize DistinctSecrets = dcount(TargetFilePath),
            SecretPaths = make_set(TargetFilePath, 20),
            ActingCmd = any(ActingProcessCommandLine),
            FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated)
  by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessId, bin(TimeGenerated, 5m)
| where DistinctSecrets >= 3
| order by LastSeen desc
(source:windows.sysmon @EventID:11 @Image:(*\\node.exe OR *\\Node.exe) @TargetFilename:(*\\.npmrc* OR *\\.aws\\credentials* OR *\\.docker\\config.json* OR *\\.ssh\\* OR *\\.config\\gh\\* OR *\\.gcloud\\* OR *\\.kube\\config*)) OR (source:linux.auditd @auditd.type:(OPEN OR OPENAT) @process.name:node @file.path:(*/.npmrc* OR */.aws/credentials* OR */.docker/config.json* OR */.ssh/* OR */.config/gh/* OR */.gcloud/* OR */.kube/config*))
#event_simpleName=/^(FileOpenInfo|FileWritten)$/ ContextImageFileName=/[\\/]node(\.exe)?$/i (TargetFileName=/\.npmrc$/i OR TargetFileName=/\.aws[\\/]credentials$/i OR TargetFileName=/\.docker[\\/]config\.json$/i OR TargetFileName=/[\\/]\.ssh[\\/]/i OR TargetFileName=/[\\/]\.config[\\/]gh[\\/]/i OR TargetFileName=/[\\/]\.gcloud[\\/]/i OR TargetFileName=/[\\/]\.kube[\\/]config$/i)
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, UserName, ContextProcessId], function={ distinct_secret_paths := count(field=TargetFileName, distinct=true) | sample_paths := collect(TargetFileName, limit=10) })
| distinct_secret_paths >= 3

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

let LookbackDays = 7d;

    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | project NetworkMessageId, RecipientEmailAddress,
              AttachmentFileName = FileName, AttachmentSHA256 = SHA256;
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("OUTLOOK.EXE","winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                      "mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe")
| join kind=inner MalAttachments on $left.AccountUpn == $right.RecipientEmailAddress
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, AttachmentFileName, AttachmentSHA256
// Sentinel doesn't have an attachment-hash table. Best approximation:
// OfficeActivity Send/Receive activity + correlate to host process spawn.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MailWithAttach = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation in~ ("Send","Receive")
    | where isnotempty(MessageId)
    | project EmailTime = TimeGenerated, MailboxOwnerUPN, Sender,
              Recipients, Subject, MessageId;
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName endswith "OUTLOOK.EXE"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'

falsepositives:

level: high

title: Remote-Management (RMM) Tool Execution

Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children Exploitation Medium
Supply-chain trojan signal: legitimate signed binary spawning script interpreters or exotic LOLBins.
ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match Installation High
Match SHA256/SHA1/MD5 named in the article against EDR file/process telemetry.
ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("46faab8ab153fae6e80e7cca38eab363075bb524edd79e42269217a083628f09") or SHA1 in~ ("46faab8ab153fae6e80e7cca38eab363075bb524edd79e42269217a083628f09") or MD5 in~ ("46faab8ab153fae6e80e7cca38eab363075bb524edd79e42269217a083628f09")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("46faab8ab153fae6e80e7cca38eab363075bb524edd79e42269217a083628f09") or SHA1 in~ ("46faab8ab153fae6e80e7cca38eab363075bb524edd79e42269217a083628f09") or MD5 in~ ("46faab8ab153fae6e80e7cca38eab363075bb524edd79e42269217a083628f09")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = ProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceProcessEvents" )
| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["46faab8ab153fae6e80e7cca38eab363075bb524edd79e42269217a083628f09"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

| order by Timestamp desc

Actions on Objectives
Likely
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code.
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s) Reconnaissance High
Match vulnerability scanner output to the CVE(s) named in the article.
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-5086")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-5086")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match Installation High
Match SHA256/SHA1/MD5 named in the article against EDR file/process telemetry.
ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("292ea9dbc5a1d15b769edb5df1602418931122455223081064ad7ea4e8ab6821") or SHA1 in~ ("292ea9dbc5a1d15b769edb5df1602418931122455223081064ad7ea4e8ab6821") or MD5 in~ ("292ea9dbc5a1d15b769edb5df1602418931122455223081064ad7ea4e8ab6821")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("292ea9dbc5a1d15b769edb5df1602418931122455223081064ad7ea4e8ab6821") or SHA1 in~ ("292ea9dbc5a1d15b769edb5df1602418931122455223081064ad7ea4e8ab6821") or MD5 in~ ("292ea9dbc5a1d15b769edb5df1602418931122455223081064ad7ea4e8ab6821")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = ProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceProcessEvents" )
| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["292ea9dbc5a1d15b769edb5df1602418931122455223081064ad7ea4e8ab6821"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

npm Supply Chain Attack via Open Source maintainer compromise

Snyk
2025-09-08 13 use cases 4 techniques 5 kill-chain phases detected

On Monday, September 8th, a highly regarded open source developer, ~qix, was compromised via a phishing email.

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Inbound phishing email from npmjs.help maintainer-takeover domain · [LLM] Browser/HTTPS traffic to npmjs.help credential-harvesting page · Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process · Fake CAPTCHA / clipboard-injected PowerShell (ClickFix / FakeCaptcha) · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children · Article-specific behavioural hunt — npm Supply Chain Attack via Open Source maintainer compromise
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Install / lockfile mention of the 28 compromised Qix-campaign package@versions
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Egress to websocket-api2.publicvm.com (Qix campaign credential exfil C2) · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → Crypto-wallet file/keystore access by non-wallet process · Infostealer — non-browser process accessing browser cookie/login DBs
Inbound phishing email from npmjs.help maintainer-takeover domain AIDelivery High
Hunts inbound mail from or linking to the look-alike domain npmjs.help that was registered 3 days before the Qix maintainer compromise and used to harvest npm credentials + 2FA codes. Targets the initial-access lure that started the entire supply-chain incident. Rationale: Pivots on the single high-fidelity IOC the article and Wiz/Sygnia/Palo Alto write-ups all corroborate: the typosquat domain npmjs.help registered 5 Sept 2025 specifically for this campaign. Any inbound mail referencing this domain is, by Snyk's own statement, the indicator of compromise for maintainers. Cross-checked against: • https://www.wiz.io/blog/widespread-npm-supply-chain-attack-breaking-down-impact-scope-across-debug-chalk • https://blog.checkpoint.com/crypto/the-great-npm-heist-september-2025/ • https://www.sygnia.co/threat-reports-and-advisories/npm-supply-chain-attack-september-2025/
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1656
Data sourcesEmail
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security-vendor threat-intel digests forwarded internally that quote the IOC string in the body
  • Internal SOC tickets/Jira emails containing the IOC for awareness
  • Maintainer-of-affected-package mailing-list discussion thread quoting the phishing domain
  • Snyk/Wiz/Checkpoint marketing emails describing the incident
  • Tabletop / phishing-simulation exercises crafted using the same lure
Suppression filter
| where not (RecipientEmailAddress has_any ("soc@","threatintel@","abuse@","phishing@")) and not (SenderFromDomain endswith "snyk.io" or SenderFromDomain endswith "wiz.io" or SenderFromDomain endswith "checkpoint.com")
Response runbook
1. Triage: pull EmailEvents row by NetworkMessageId, confirm DeliveryLocation (Inbox vs Junk vs Quarantine), check if URL was clicked via UrlClickEvents on the recipient UPN. Inspect SenderIPv4 + SenderFromDomain to distinguish forwarded threat-intel from a live lure. 2. Contain: if Delivery=Inbox and the recipient is an npm maintainer or anyone with publish rights to internal-owned packages, force-reset their npm token, rotate any GitHub-linked 2FA seeds, and quarantine the mail via Threat Explorer ZAP. 3. Hunt: in DeviceNetworkEvents look for outbound HTTPS to npmjs.help from any host belonging to the recipient or anyone CC'd, plus credential-exfil shape to websocket-api2.publicvm.com (covered by the C2 UC). Audit npm publish events on internal registries for the prior 7 days.

    | where Timestamp > ago(lookback)
    | where UrlDomain endswith badDomain or Url has badDomain
    | project Timestamp, NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain;
let senders = EmailEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(lookback)
    | where SenderFromDomain endswith badDomain
         or SenderMailFromDomain endswith badDomain
         or SenderFromAddress has "@npmjs.help"
    | project Timestamp, NetworkMessageId, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress, Subject, DeliveryAction, DeliveryLocation;
senders

         | project Timestamp, NetworkMessageId, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress, Subject, DeliveryAction, DeliveryLocation, Url, UrlDomain)
| order by Timestamp desc

Browser/HTTPS traffic to npmjs.help credential-harvesting page AIDelivery High
Catches any host that resolved or fetched the npmjs.help phishing site that masqueraded as npm login + 2FA. Anyone hitting this domain has either typed credentials into the attacker page or is about to. Rationale: Three independently-corroborated IOCs (Wiz, Checkpoint, Sygnia) — the typosquat domain plus the two BunnyCDN buckets that served the fake-login JavaScript — pinned to network telemetry. Adds the resolved-IP 185.7.81.108 from Checkpoint's analysis so the rule still catches direct-IP fallback. Cross-checked against: • https://www.wiz.io/blog/widespread-npm-supply-chain-attack-breaking-down-impact-scope-across-debug-chalk • https://blog.checkpoint.com/crypto/the-great-npm-heist-september-2025/ • https://www.paloaltonetworks.com/blog/cloud-security/npm-supply-chain-attack/
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1056.003
Data sourcesWeb Network_Resolution
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security researchers visiting the IOC from a sandbox / vuln-research VM
  • DFIR responders manually browsing the domain during triage of this very incident
  • Threat-intel tooling auto-fetching the IOC for enrichment (URLScan, VirusTotal, Hybrid Analysis pivots)
  • Detonation by EDR cloud-detonation services that originate from corporate egress
  • Outbound queries from awareness-training simulations that include the live domain string
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("msedge.exe","chrome.exe","firefox.exe") or InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has_any ("--headless","urlscan","virustotal","hybrid-analysis","sandbox")
Response runbook
1. Triage: identify the user behind the device (InitiatingProcessAccountUpn / SrcUsername). Pull the surrounding 60-minute browser history; look for an HTTP POST to npmjs.help carrying credentials. Check if the user holds npm publish rights, GitHub maintainer status, or owns internal package registries. 2. Contain: force npm token rotation (`npm token revoke`), reset npm + GitHub passwords, invalidate active GitHub PATs, re-enroll 2FA. Block the three domains and 185.7.81.108 at the proxy/firewall. 3. Hunt: scan the user's workstation and any CI runners they touch for outbound connections to websocket-api2.publicvm.com (the C2 IOC) and for the `_0x112fa8` obfuscation marker inside files under node_modules/.
let badHosts = dynamic(["npmjs.help","static-mw-host.b-cdn.net","img-data-backup.b-cdn.net"]);

      or RemoteIP == "185.7.81.108"

let webHits = ImWebSession
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(90d)
    | where HttpHostName has_any (badHosts) or Url has_any (badHosts)
    | project TimeGenerated, SrcIpAddr, SrcHostname, SrcUsername, HttpHostName, Url, HttpStatusCode, HttpMethod;
