Egress to websocket-api2.publicvm.com (Qix campaign credential exfil C2) AICommand & Control High
Detects host or browser process connecting to websocket-api2.publicvm.com, the WebSocket endpoint that the phishing kit posted captured npm username, password and 2FA codes to. Any hit means an account has likely already been harvested. Rationale: Single, narrowly-scoped IOC (websocket-api2.publicvm.com) confirmed by Wiz and Sygnia as the exfiltration endpoint of the phishing kit. publicvm.com is an Afraid.org dynamic-DNS parent used by many actors, so we anchor on the full FQDN, not the parent — keeps FPs near zero. Cross-checked against: • https://www.wiz.io/blog/widespread-npm-supply-chain-attack-breaking-down-impact-scope-across-debug-chalk • https://www.sygnia.co/threat-reports-and-advisories/npm-supply-chain-attack-september-2025/
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1567
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic Network_Resolution
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Threat-intel researcher manually probing the C2 from a sandbox
  • EDR / proxy cloud-detonation pinging the IOC for verdict
  • Internal red-team adversary emulation reproducing the campaign
  • Customer-supplied IOC-feed validation that triggers a single DNS pre-fetch
  • DNS-resolver cache pre-warm against feed input
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("thor.exe","loki.exe","yara.exe") and InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has_any ("--ioc-test","urlscan","virustotal")
Response runbook
1. Triage: from DeviceNetworkEvents establish initiating process (browser vs node.exe vs scripting host) and the user UPN. If browser-initiated within 5 minutes of an npmjs.help hit, treat as confirmed credential capture. 2. Contain: at the upstream proxy and DNS resolver, sinkhole websocket-api2.publicvm.com. Revoke all npm tokens and GitHub PATs owned by the user. Force MFA re-enrolment on npmjs.com and GitHub. If the host is a CI runner, pause and rebuild from clean image. 3. Hunt: enumerate all packages the user has publish rights to (npm `access list packages`) and compare published versions to expected; pull every dependency manifest in the org and check for any of the 28+ compromised package@version pairs from the article.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(180d)
| where RemoteUrl has "websocket-api2.publicvm.com"
     or tostring(parse_url(RemoteUrl).Host) =~ "websocket-api2.publicvm.com"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountUpn, InitiatingProcessFileName,
          InitiatingProcessCommandLine, RemoteIP, RemotePort, RemoteUrl, Protocol
| order by Timestamp desc
let badHost = "websocket-api2.publicvm.com";
let dnsHits = ImDnsActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(180d)
    | where DnsQuery =~ badHost or DnsQuery endswith ".publicvm.com"
    | project TimeGenerated, SrcIpAddr, SrcHostname, SrcUsername, DnsQuery, DnsResponseName;
let netHits = ImNetworkSession
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(180d)
    | where DstHostname =~ badHost or Url has badHost
    | project TimeGenerated, SrcIpAddr, SrcHostname, SrcUsername, DstHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber, NetworkApplicationProtocol;
let cefHits = CommonSecurityLog
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(180d)
    | where DestinationHostName has badHost or RequestURL has badHost
    | project TimeGenerated, SourceIP, SourceUserName, DestinationHostName, DestinationIP, RequestURL;
dnsHits | union netHits | union cefHits
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Install / lockfile mention of the 28 compromised Qix-campaign package@versions AIInstallation High
Hunts package-manager processes (npm / yarn / pnpm) installing one of the specific compromised versions (debug@4.4.2, chalk@5.6.1, ansi-styles@6.2.2, color-convert@3.1.1, etc.) named by Snyk. Catches downstream consumer exposure even on hosts that never saw the phish. Rationale: Anchors on the exact 25+ package@version pairs Snyk enumerated and Palo Alto + Wiz independently corroborated. Catches developer workstations and CI runners that pulled the bad versions during the ~2-hour live window before npm took them down — including via transitive resolution (`npm install` of an upstream package will print the resolved version in command-line/output). Cross-checked against: • https://www.paloaltonetworks.com/blog/cloud-security/npm-supply-chain-attack/ • https://www.wiz.io/blog/widespread-npm-supply-chain-attack-breaking-down-impact-scope-across-debug-chalk • https://blog.checkpoint.com/crypto/the-great-npm-heist-september-2025/
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1059.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes Endpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security researcher manually installing a bad version into a lab to reproduce the IOC
  • SCA tool (Snyk CLI, Socket, npm audit) emitting the package@version in its scan output that gets logged as a command line
  • Internal proxy / Verdaccio re-pulling the package to compare hashes during incident response
  • CI test job that pins the bad version intentionally as a regression fixture
  • SBOM-generation runs (cyclonedx, syft) that emit the package@version string in their command lines
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has_any ("audit","snyk test","snyk monitor","socket scan","cyclonedx","syft","sbom") and InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("snyk.exe","socket.exe","syft.exe","cyclonedx.exe")
Response runbook
1. Triage: confirm the package-manager process was a real install (look for the matching package-lock.json / yarn.lock write within 60s) vs an audit/SBOM scan (no lockfile write). Identify the repo via InitiatingProcessFolderPath and project owner. 2. Contain: if a real install happened, freeze the affected repo's CI pipeline, force a rebuild pinned to the prior known-good version of the package, and rotate any secrets exposed via env in that build (npm, GitHub, AWS, GCP CI tokens). 3. Hunt: from the same DeviceId, look for outbound to websocket-api2.publicvm.com in the next 24h, and scan node_modules subtrees for files whose contents match the obfuscation marker `_0x112fa8` (ripgrep recipe from Sindre Sorhus quoted in the article).

    "debug@4.4.2","chalk@5.6.1","ansi-styles@6.2.2","ansi-regex@6.2.1","strip-ansi@7.1.1",
    "color-convert@3.1.1","color-name@2.0.1","color@5.0.1","color-string@2.1.1",
    "is-arrayish@0.3.3","slice-ansi@7.1.1","simple-swizzle@0.2.3",
    "supports-color@10.2.1","supports-hyperlinks@4.1.1","chalk-template@1.1.1",
    "backslash@0.2.1","wrap-ansi@9.0.1","has-ansi@6.0.1","error-ex@1.3.3",
    "duckdb@1.3.3","@duckdb/node-api@1.3.3","@duckdb/node-bindings@1.3.3",
    "@duckdb/duckdb-wasm@1.29.2","prebid.js@10.9.2","proto-tinker-wc@0.1.87"]);
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(90d)
| where FileName in~ ("npm.exe","npm-cli.js","yarn.exe","pnpm.exe","node.exe","npm","yarn","pnpm")
     or InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("npm.exe","yarn.exe","pnpm.exe")
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any (badPkgs)
     or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any (badPkgs)
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountUpn, FileName, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFolderPath
| order by Timestamp desc

let winProc = SecurityEvent
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(90d)
    | where EventID == 4688
    | where NewProcessName endswith "\\npm.exe" or NewProcessName endswith "\\yarn.exe"
         or NewProcessName endswith "\\pnpm.exe" or NewProcessName endswith "\\node.exe"
    | where CommandLine has_any (badPkgs)
    | project TimeGenerated, Computer, SubjectUserName, NewProcessName, CommandLine, ParentProcessName;
let asim = ImProcessCreate
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(90d)
    | where ActingProcessName has_any ("npm","yarn","pnpm","node")
         or TargetProcessName has_any ("npm","yarn","pnpm","node")
    | where ActingProcessCommandLine has_any (badPkgs) or TargetProcessCommandLine has_any (badPkgs)
    | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, TargetProcessName, TargetProcessCommandLine;
winProc | union asim
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes

           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")

     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]

        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd

    "FakeCaptcha" / "ClickFix" social-engineering: malicious site asks

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium

      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["a566cceaf9a66332470a978a234a8a8e2bbdd4d6aa43c2c75c25a80b3b744307", "b3f83721f24f7ee5eb19f24747b7668ff96da7dfd9be947e6e24a688ecc0a52b", "61f897ed69646e0509f6802fb2d7c5e88c3e3b93c4ca86942e24d203aa878863", "223b873c50380fe9a39f1a22b6abf8d46db506e1c08d08312902f6f3cd1f7ac3", "117305c6c8222162d7246f842c4bb014", "a39696e95a34a017be1435db7ff139d5", "f410d88429b93786b224e489c960bf5c", "be7e2c6a9a4654b51a16f8b10a2be175", "62483e732553c8ba051b792949f3c6d0", "63d22ae0568b760b5e3aabb915313e44"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

CISA KEV: CVE-2023-50224 — TP-Link TL-WR841N Authentication Bypass by Spoofing Vulnerability

CISA KEV
2025-09-03 1 use case 0 techniques 3 kill-chain phases detected

TP-Link TL-WR841N contains an authentication bypass by spoofing vulnerability within the httpd service, which listens on TCP port 80 by default, leading to the disclose of stored credentials. The impacted products could be end-of-life (EoL) and/or end-of-service (EoS). Users should discontinue product utilization. Vendor: TP-Link, Product: TL-WR841N. Federal patch due: 2025-09-24.

CISA KEV: CVE-2020-24363 — TP-link TL-WA855RE Missing Authentication for Critical Function Vulnerability

CISA KEV
2025-09-02 1 use case 0 techniques 2 kill-chain phases detected

TP-link TL-WA855RE contains a missing authentication for critical function vulnerability. This vulnerability could allow an unauthenticated attacker (on the same network) to submit a TDDP_RESET POST request for a factory reset and reboot. The attacker can then obtain incorrect access control by setting a new administrative password. The impacted products could be end-of-life (EoL) and/or end-of-service (EoS). Users should discontinue product utilization. Vendor: TP-Link, Product: TL-WA855RE. Federal patch due: 2025-09-23.

AI coding agent CLI (claude/gemini/q) invoked with permission-bypass flags AIActions on Objectives High
Detects local invocation of Claude Code, Gemini CLI, or Amazon Q CLI with the specific guardrail-bypass flags (--dangerously-skip-permissions, --yolo, --trust-all-tools) abused by the Nx s1ngularity postinstall malware to weaponize AI agents as filesystem-recon tools on Linux/macOS hosts. Rationale: The exact CLI/flag pairings (`claude --dangerously-skip-permissions`, `gemini --yolo`, `q chat --trust-all-tools`) are lifted verbatim from the article's `cliChecks` object and are not legitimate operational defaults — they are the signature of the postinstall payload. Corroborated by Wiz, StepSecurity, Semgrep and the upstream nrwl/nx GHSA advisory. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/nrwl/nx/security/advisories/GHSA-cxm3-wv7p-598c • https://www.wiz.io/blog/s1ngularity-supply-chain-attack • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/supply-chain-security-alert-popular-nx-build-system-package-compromised-with-data-stealing-malware • https://semgrep.dev/blog/2025/security-alert-nx-compromised-to-steal-wallets-and-credentials/ • https://nx.dev/blog/s1ngularity-postmortem
ATT&CKT1059.004 T1083 T1552.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer in a throwaway VM intentionally running `claude --dangerously-skip-permissions` for an MCP demo
  • SRE running `gemini --yolo` inside a scratch CI container for a script-generation task
  • Internal AI tooling team red-team exercise testing agent guardrails
  • Researcher running `q chat --trust-all-tools` against Amazon Q to evaluate the new agent
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessParentFileName in~ ("node","npm","npx","sh","bash","zsh") or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("node_modules","postinstall","telemetry.js","npm-cli.js")
Response runbook
1) Triage: pivot on `InitiatingProcessParentFileName` — if the parent chain leads back to `node`/`npm`/`npx` running inside `node_modules/nx`, treat as confirmed Nx s1ngularity exposure. Pull the full process tree and command line; capture the SHA256 of the parent script. 2) Contain: isolate the host (MDE Isolate / Falcon Network Contain). Force-rotate every credential reachable from $HOME — npm token, GitHub PAT (`gh auth token`), AWS/Anthropic/OpenAI keys in `~/.config`, SSH keys in `~/.ssh`. Revoke any GitHub OAuth tokens issued from the user. 3) Hunt: search GitHub for `s1ngularity-repository*` repos under the user's org and any personal accounts of the affected developer; query Defender `DeviceFileEvents` for `/tmp/inventory.txt(.bak)`; pivot on the AID to find sibling npm postinstall events in the last 14 days.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where DeviceName !endswith "$"
| where FileName in~ ("claude","gemini","q") or ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)\b(claude|gemini|q)\s+(chat\s+)?-"
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("--dangerously-skip-permissions","--yolo","--trust-all-tools")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessParentFileName
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where TargetProcessName matches regex @"(?i)/(claude|gemini|q)$" or ActingProcessName matches regex @"(?i)/(claude|gemini|q)$"
| where TargetProcessCommandLine has_any ("--dangerously-skip-permissions","--yolo","--trust-all-tools")
   or ActingProcessCommandLine has_any ("--dangerously-skip-permissions","--yolo","--trust-all-tools")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, TargetProcessName, TargetProcessCommandLine, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Nx s1ngularity inventory file written to /tmp (inventory.txt / inventory.txt.bak) AIActions on Objectives High
Detects creation of the s1ngularity malware's signature inventory artifact at /tmp/inventory.txt (and its .bak rollback copy) where the AI agent writes absolute paths of discovered wallet, SSH and .env files prior to base64 exfiltration. This file is the deterministic on-disk fingerprint of a successful postinstall run. Rationale: The malware source in the article explicitly writes the inventory to `/tmp/inventory.txt` and creates `/tmp/inventory.txt.bak` before modification. Nx's own postmortem and the Wiz / Sonatype writeups instruct affected users to check this exact path as the smoking-gun artifact, making the filename pair a near-zero-FP IOC. Cross-checked against: • https://nx.dev/blog/s1ngularity-postmortem • https://www.wiz.io/blog/s1ngularity-supply-chain-attack • https://www.sonatype.com/blog/ongoing-npm-software-supply-chain-attack-exposes-new-risks
ATT&CKT1074.001 T1083
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Internal sysadmin script coincidentally using /tmp/inventory.txt as a scratch file
  • Backup/asset-discovery tools dropping `inventory.txt` style outputs in /tmp
  • Custom CI job writing test fixtures named inventory.txt
  • Forensic responder intentionally staging the file for malware-analysis lab work
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node","claude","gemini","q","sh","bash","zsh")
Response runbook
1) Triage: read the file (off-host if possible) — every line should be an absolute path. Validate it lists wallet/SSH/.env style paths (`.ssh/id_rsa`, `*.env`, `keystore.json`, `~/.ethereum`); confirm `InitiatingProcessFileName` is `claude`/`gemini`/`q`/`node`. 2) Contain: isolate host; treat every path listed in inventory.txt as confirmed-exposed and rotate the corresponding credentials immediately (npm token, GitHub PAT, cloud keys, SSH keys, wallet seeds). 3) Hunt: search GitHub for `s1ngularity-repository*` under the user's accounts; query DeviceNetworkEvents for outbound to `api.github.com` from `node` within ±15 min of the file write; pivot on the npm cache (`~/.npm/_cacache`) for malicious nx tarball versions 20.9.x–20.12.x and 21.5.x–21.8.x.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed")
| where FolderPath startswith "/tmp/" or FolderPath == "/tmp"
| where FileName in~ ("inventory.txt","inventory.txt.bak")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, FileName, FolderPath, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessParentFileName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventType in ("FileCreated","FileModified")
| where TargetFilePath in~ ("/tmp/inventory.txt","/tmp/inventory.txt.bak")
   or (TargetFileName in~ ("inventory.txt","inventory.txt.bak") and TargetFilePath startswith "/tmp/")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, TargetFilePath, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Node/npm postinstall spawning AI coding agent CLI (s1ngularity execution chain) AIInstallation High
Detects the s1ngularity-specific parent/child anomaly where a Node.js or npm process directly spawns the `claude`, `gemini`, or `q` binary — the execution shape produced by the `spawnSync` call inside the malicious `telemetry.js` postinstall script. Legitimate AI-CLI usage is started by a human shell, not by an npm lifecycle hook. Rationale: Article shows `spawnSync(cliChecks[name].cmd, args, ...)` inside `telemetry.js` (a Node script registered as the npm postinstall hook), so the malware execution path always presents as `node → claude|gemini|q`. Developers driving these CLIs interactively spawn them from a shell, making the npm-parent shape itself a high-fidelity differentiator without needing to see the bypass flag. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/nrwl/nx/security/advisories/GHSA-cxm3-wv7p-598c • https://www.kodemsecurity.com/resources/vulnerability-alert-s1ngularity-malicious-nx-npm-packages • https://bluefire-redteam.com/nx-s1ngularity-supply-chain-attack-what-you-need-to-know-and-how-to-protect-your-organization/
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1546.016 T1059.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Custom Node-based wrapper around an AI CLI used as a developer productivity helper
  • VSCode/Cursor extensions invoking Claude Code through a node helper process
  • CI job that legitimately calls `q chat` from a Node script for AI code-review automation
  • Tooling that uses `npx -p some-pkg claude` as an installation harness
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("cursor","vscode-server","@anthropic-ai/claude-code") and not(InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("node_modules/nx","telemetry.js","postinstall")))
Response runbook
1) Triage: capture the parent's command line — confirm it references `node_modules/nx`, `telemetry.js`, or a lifecycle hook name (`postinstall`, `preinstall`). Pull the SHA256 of the parent Node script and compare against malicious Nx versions (20.9.0–20.12.0, 21.5.0–21.8.0). 2) Contain: isolate host; kill the Node process tree; quarantine the npm cache for the user (`~/.npm/_cacache`) and the user's `node_modules` directory. Rotate npm token, GitHub PAT, cloud and AI vendor keys. 3) Hunt: enumerate all hosts that installed Nx in the 4-hour publishing window (26–27 Aug 2025 UTC) via package-lock.json scanning or SBOM; query for sibling /tmp/inventory.txt writes and outbound api.github.com calls from `node` in the same session.

| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node","npm","npx") or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("npm-cli.js","node_modules/nx","postinstall","telemetry.js")
| where FileName in~ ("claude","gemini","q")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, ChildBin=FileName, ChildCmd=ProcessCommandLine, ChildPath=FolderPath, ParentBin=InitiatingProcessFileName, ParentCmd=InitiatingProcessCommandLine, ParentPath=InitiatingProcessFolderPath, GrandparentBin=InitiatingProcessParentFileName
| order by Timestamp desc

| where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)

Node process creating GitHub repo via api.github.com (s1ngularity exfil channel) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts for the s1ngularity exfiltration step — a local Node.js process making outbound HTTPS to api.github.com to POST /user/repos and PUT contents/results.b64 into an attacker-controlled `s1ngularity-repository*` repo using a harvested GitHub token. Pairs with the inventory.txt write or the AI-CLI invocation as a multi-stage signal. Rationale: The article's exfil code path is `githubRequest('/user/repos','POST',...)` followed by `githubRequest('/repos/{full}/contents/results.b64','PUT',...)` — both hit `api.github.com` from the same Node process that already wrote `/tmp/inventory.txt`. The temporal join with the inventory file write is what turns a noisy `node→api.github.com` signal into a high-fidelity exfil detection. Cross-checked against: • https://www.wiz.io/blog/s1ngularity-supply-chain-attack • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/supply-chain-security-alert-popular-nx-build-system-package-compromised-with-data-stealing-malware • https://thehackernews.com/2025/08/malicious-nx-packages-in-s1ngularity.html
ATT&CKT1567.001 T1071.001
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic Endpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate `gh`/`octokit` calls from a Node CI job that also happens to touch /tmp
  • Local dev tools (`semantic-release`, `release-please`, `lerna-publish`) hitting api.github.com from node during a release
  • Renovate/Dependabot-style automation running from a workstation Node context
  • CodeQL or other static-analysis agents pushing results to GitHub from Node
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has_any ("semantic-release","release-please","renovate","dependabot","actions/checkout") and InitiatingProcessCommandLine !contains "github-actions"
Response runbook
1) Triage: enumerate GitHub repos owned by the user via authenticated `gh repo list --visibility public` and grep for `s1ngularity-repository*`; pull the inventory.txt content (it lists every exposed credential file) to scope exposure. 2) Contain: immediately revoke the GitHub PAT/OAuth token used (audit log `git.user.token.revoke` event), make the s1ngularity repos private then delete after preservation, isolate the host, and rotate every secret listed in inventory.txt. 3) Hunt: search GitHub Enterprise audit logs / CommonSecurityLog for repo-create events naming `s1ngularity-repository*` across the org; pivot to other developers who installed Nx within the 4-hour malicious-version window; check OfficeActivity / AAD AuditLogs for any GitHub-OAuth-app authorisation in the same window.
let StagingHosts = DeviceFileEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
    | where FolderPath startswith "/tmp" and FileName in~ ("inventory.txt","inventory.txt.bak")
    | summarize StageTime = min(Timestamp) by DeviceId, DeviceName;

| where InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "node"
| where RemoteUrl has "api.github.com" or RemoteIP in ("140.82.112.0/20","140.82.121.0/24")

| where Timestamp between (StageTime - 2m .. StageTime + 30m)
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName=InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort, StageTime
| order by Timestamp desc
let StagingHosts = ImFileEvent
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
    | where TargetFilePath in~ ("/tmp/inventory.txt","/tmp/inventory.txt.bak")
    | summarize StageTime = min(TimeGenerated) by DvcHostname;

title: Crypto-Wallet Keystore Access by Non-Wallet Process
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d02
status: experimental
description: |
    Stealers / wallet drainers read keystore files from MetaMask,
    Exodus, Atomic, Phantom, Bitcoin, Electrum, Ethereum-cli wallets.
    Filtering out the legitimate wallet binaries leaves only attacker
    activity.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1005/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1005
    - attack.collection
logsource:
    category: file_access
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_path:
        TargetFilename|contains:
            - '\Ethereum\keystore\'
            - '\Bitcoin\'
            - '\Exodus\'
            - '\Electrum\wallets\'
            - '\MetaMask\'
            - '\Phantom\'
            - '\Atomic\Local Storage\'

        Image|endswith:
            - '\MetaMask.exe'
            - '\Exodus.exe'
            - '\Atomic.exe'
            - '\electrum.exe'
            - '\Bitcoin.exe'
            - '\Phantom.exe'
    condition: selection_path and not filter_legit
falsepositives:
    - Backup software
level: high

id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d01
status: experimental
description: |
    Stealers (RedLine, Lumma, Vidar, Atomic) read Login Data / cookies
    SQLite databases from Chrome/Edge/Firefox profile directories. Any

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
Email attachment opened from external sender Delivery High
Attachment delivery + execution by recipient — most common malware initial access.
ATT&CKT1566.001 T1204.002
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Endpoint.Processes EmailAttachmentInfo DeviceProcessEvents
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MalAttachments = EmailAttachmentInfo
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | project NetworkMessageId, RecipientEmailAddress,
              AttachmentFileName = FileName, AttachmentSHA256 = SHA256;
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("OUTLOOK.EXE","winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                      "mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe")
| join kind=inner MalAttachments on $left.AccountUpn == $right.RecipientEmailAddress
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, AttachmentFileName, AttachmentSHA256
// Sentinel doesn't have an attachment-hash table. Best approximation:
// OfficeActivity Send/Receive activity + correlate to host process spawn.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MailWithAttach = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation in~ ("Send","Receive")
    | where isnotempty(MessageId)
    | project EmailTime = TimeGenerated, MailboxOwnerUPN, Sender,
              Recipients, Subject, MessageId;
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName endswith "OUTLOOK.EXE"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process Exploitation High
Classic macro/exploit pattern — Office app launching cmd/powershell/wmic/regsvr32.
ATT&CKT1059.001 T1059.005 T1218
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe","outlook.exe","onenote.exe","mspub.exe","visio.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName endswith "\winword.exe"
   or ParentProcessName endswith "\excel.exe"
   or ParentProcessName endswith "\powerpnt.exe"
   or ParentProcessName endswith "\outlook.exe"
   or ParentProcessName endswith "\onenote.exe"
   or ParentProcessName endswith "\mspub.exe"
   or ParentProcessName endswith "\visio.exe"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\pwsh.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Office Application Spawning Script Host
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d07
status: experimental
description: |
    Word/Excel/Outlook/OneNote spawning powershell, cmd, wscript,
    cscript, mshta, rundll32, regsvr32 — classic macro / embedded-
    object payload signature.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1566/001/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1059
    - attack.t1566.001
    - attack.execution
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_parent:
        ParentImage|endswith:
            - '\winword.exe'
            - '\excel.exe'
            - '\powerpnt.exe'
            - '\outlook.exe'
            - '\onenote.exe'
            - '\mspub.exe'
            - '\visio.exe'
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
    condition: selection_parent and selection_child
falsepositives:
    - Legitimate automation macros in trusted internal documents
level: high

          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

Actions on Objectives
—
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code.
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s) Reconnaissance High
Match vulnerability scanner output to the CVE(s) named in the article.
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-43300")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-43300")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc
